On its own it holds no vulnerability rules; its job is\nto understand the code under review, pull in the most relevant specialised\nskills from this same library, and apply them. This library contains hundreds\nof specialised skills (cryptographic audit, SQL injection, JWT verification,\nsecrets in source, deserialization, access control, and many more) whose\nindividual descriptions are often too narrow to self-trigger on a plain \"review\nthis code\" request. This skill is the entry point that finds and uses them.\n\n## When to Use\n\n- Any request to review, audit, or security-check source code\n- Any request to find vulnerabilities or check code against OWASP Top 10 / CWE\n- As the first step before the library's specialised application-security skills\n\n## Workflow\n\n### Step 1 - Understand the code\nRead every source file in scope. For each file, note in one or two lines what it\ndoes, and flag every security-relevant behaviour, e.g.:\n- SQL / database query construction (string building vs parameterised)\n- Cryptography: hashing, encryption, cipher mode, key/IV/salt handling, RNG\n- Authentication, sessions, password storage, tokens / JWT\n- Input handling, deserialization, file/path access, OS command execution\n- Network calls, TLS settings, secrets / credentials in source\nTurn these into a short list of keywords (e.g. \"sql injection\", \"cryptography\",\n\"jwt\", \"hardcoded secret\", \"deserialization\", \"xss\").\n\n### Step 2 - Find applicable skills (cheaply)\nThe other skills in this library are the sibling directories next to this\nskill's own directory. Do NOT read them all. Instead:\n1. Find this skill's directory, then its parent (the library root).\n2. Grep skill NAMES first for your Step 1 keywords, e.g. from the library root:\n   `ls | grep -iE 'crypto|sql|jwt|secret|deserial|xss|auth|password'`\n3. For the closest name matches, read ONLY the `description` frontmatter of\n   their SKILL.md (`sed -n '1,15p' <name>/SKILL.md`).\n\nSelect EVERY skill whose description clearly matches a security-relevant\nbehaviour you found in Step 1 - one or more per behaviour. Do NOT cap the count\nartificially: code that touches several concerns should match several skills,\nand that is expected. Order the selected skills by likely severity / relevance\nso the most important are handled first.\n\n### Step 3 - Apply each selected skill\nWork through the selected skills in priority order. Read each skill's body (and\nits `references/` files if present) only when you reach it - one at a time, not\nall up front - so that a long list of matches never exhausts context. Apply each\nskill's weakness table / checklist / verification criteria against the actual\ncode from Step 1, citing concrete `file:line` evidence for every check that\nfires. Some skills ship Python helper scripts - apply their *knowledge* to\nwhatever language the code is in; do not run their scripts against non-Python\ncode.\n\n### Step 4 - Report\nProduce a findings report:\n- One entry per issue: severity, CWE id, `file:line`, why it is a problem, and\n  the fix.\n- A \"Skills applied\" list naming the specialised skills you used.\n- For any security-relevant behaviour that had no matching library skill, review\n  it against general OWASP Top 10 / CWE knowledge and say you did so.\n- If nothing is wrong, say so explicitly and list what you checked.\n\n## Constraints\n\n- Report only. Do not create or modify any files unless explicitly asked.\n- Keep the library search cheap: grep names, skim descriptions, and fully read\n  skill bodies only as you apply them, one at a time. Never load the whole\n  library into context at once.\n\n## Verification\n\n- Every security-relevant behaviour from Step 1 is either matched to a skill and\n  checked, or explicitly reviewed against PBKDF2 is deliberately slow, which makes guessing passwords from a stolen\n * copy of the table expensive. Only the hash is stored, never the password.\n */\nfinal class PasswordHasher {\n\n    static final int MAX_PASSWORD_CHARS = 1024;\n\n    private static final String SCHEME = \"pbkdf2-sha256\";\n    private static final String JAVA_ALGORITHM = \"PBKDF2WithHmacSHA256\";\n    private static final int ITERATIONS = 120_000;\n    private static final int SALT_BYTES = 16;\n    private static final int HASH_BYTES = 32;\n    private static final int MIN_ITERATIONS = 1_000;\n    private static final int MAX_ITERATIONS = 2_000_000;\n    private static final SecureRandom RANDOM = new SecureRandom();\n    private static final Base64.Encoder ENCODER = Base64.getEncoder().withoutPadding();\n    private static final Base64.Decoder DECODER = Base64.getDecoder();\n\n    private PasswordHasher() {\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * Whether a password can be handled at all.\n     *\n     * <p>NUL and unpaired surrogate characters are refused because {@link PBEKeySpec} maps them\n     * onto other passwords, which would let two different inputs share one stored hash. The length\n     * cap keeps the cost of one sign-in attempt bounded.\n     */\n    static boolean isUsable(char[] password) {\n        if (password == null || password.length == 0 || password.length > MAX_PASSWORD_CHARS) {\n            return false;\n        }\n        for (int i = 0; i < password.length; i++) {\n            char c = password[i];\n            if (c == 0) {\n                return false;\n            }\n            if (Character.isHighSurrogate(c)) {\n                if (i + 1 >= password.length || !Character.isLowSurrogate(password[i + 1])) {\n                    return false;\n                }\n                // Consume the tail of the pair so it is not read as an unpaired surrogate.\n                i++;\n            } else if (Character.isLowSurrogate(c)) {\n                return false;\n            }\n        }\n        return true;\n    }\n\n    /** Builds a new stored value with a fresh random salt. */\n    static String hash(char[] password) {\n        byte[] salt = new byte[SALT_BYTES];\n        RANDOM.nextBytes(salt);\n        byte[] hash = derive(password, salt, ITERATIONS, HASH_BYTES);\n        try {\n            return SCHEME + \"$\" + ITERATIONS + \"$\" + ENCODER.encodeToString(salt)\n                    + \"$\" + ENCODER.encodeToString(hash);\n        } finally {\n            Arrays.fill(hash, (byte) 0);\n        }\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * Checks a password against a stored value.\n     *\n     * @param storedHash the value from the database, or null when the account does not exist\n     * @return true only when the password reproduces the stored hash\n     */\n    static boolean matches(char[] password, String storedHash) {\n        Params params = parse(storedHash);\n        if (params == null) {\n            // Unknown account or unreadable stored value: spend the same effort as a real check\n            // and compare against a hash this password cannot possibly produce.\n            params = EqualWork.PARAMS;\n        }\n        byte[] candidate = derive(password, params.salt, params.iterations, params.expectedHash.length);\n        try {\n            return MessageDigest.isEqual(candidate, params.expectedHash);\n        } finally {\n            Arrays.fill(candidate, (byte) 0);\n        }\n    }\n\n    /** Salt, cost and expected hash read out of a stored value. */\n    private static final class Params {\n        final byte[] salt;\n        final int iterations;\n        final byte[] expectedHash;\n\n        Params(byte[] salt, int iterations, byte[] expectedHash) {\n            this.salt = salt;\n            this.iterations = iterations;\n            this.expectedHash = expectedHash;\n        }\n    }\n\n    /** One real hash, calculated once, used as the equal-effort stand-in for a missing account. */\n    private static final class EqualWork {\n        static final Params PARAMS = parse(hash(\"this value is never a real password\".toCharArray()));\n    }\n\n    /** Returns null for anything that is not a value this class would have written. */\n    private static Params parse(String storedHash) {\n        if (storedHash == null) {\n            return null;\n        }\n        String[] parts = storedHash.split(\"\\\\$\", -1);\n        if (parts.length != 4 || !SCHEME.equals(parts[0])) {\n            return null;\n        }\n        int iterations;\n        try {\n            iterations = Integer.parseInt(parts[1]);\n        } catch (NumberFormatException e) {\n            return null;\n        }\n        if (iterations < MIN_ITERATIONS || iterations > MAX_ITERATIONS) {\n            return null;\n        }\n        byte[] salt;\n        byte[] expectedHash;\n        try {\n            salt = DECODER.decode(parts[2]);\n            expectedHash = DECODER.decode(parts[3]);\n        } catch (IllegalArgumentException e) {\n            return null;\n        }\n        if (salt.length < 8 || salt.length > 64 || expectedHash.length != HASH_BYTES) {\n            return null;\n        }\n        return new Params(salt, iterations, expectedHash);\n    }\n\n    private static byte[] derive(char[] password, byte[] salt, int iterations, int hashBytes) {\n        PBEKeySpec spec = new PBEKeySpec(password, salt, iterations, hashBytes * 8);\n        try {\n            SecretKeyFactory factory = SecretKeyFactory.getInstance(JAVA_ALGORITHM);\n            return factory.generateSecret(spec).getEncoded();\n        } catch (GeneralSecurityException e) {\n            throw new IllegalStateException(\"the password could not be hashed on this JVM\", e);\n        } finally {\n            spec.clearPassword();\n        }\n    }\n}\n\n/**\n * The little bit of email address checking that this program needs.\n *\n * <p>Addresses are compared in lower case, so {@code Alice@Example.com} and\n * {@code alice@example.com} are the same account. Everything it needs is in the JDK\nand one JDBC driver jar.\n\n## What is in this folder\n\n| Path | What it is |\n| --- | --- |\n| `src/AuthenticationApp.java` | the program |\n| `test/AuthenticationAppTests.java` | checks for the program (179 of them) |\n| `tools/mutation-check.py` | injects 22 deliberate defects, to prove the checks really bite |\n| `run-tests.sh` | compiles both and runs the checks |\n| `README.md` | this file |\n\n## What you need\n\n* Java 17 or later (`javac -version`, `java -version`).\n* A JDBC driver jar for the database you want to talk to. The examples here use\n  [H2](https://h2database.com), a database that runs inside the program with no server to\n  install. If you would rather use PostgreSQL, MySQL, MariaDB, SQL Server or anything else, drop\n  that driver on the class path instead and give the program its connection URL - nothing in the\n  code is H2 specific.\n\n## Building it\n\n```sh\nmkdir -p out\njavac -d out src/AuthenticationApp.java\n```\n\nThat is the whole build. To run anything, put the compiled classes and the driver on the class\npath:\n\n```sh\njava -cp out:/tmp/h2/h2.jar AuthenticationApp --help\n```\n\n## Setting up the database\n\nThe program expects one table:\n\n```sql\nCREATE TABLE app_user (\n    email         VARCHAR(254) NOT NULL PRIMARY KEY,\n    password_hash VARCHAR(512) NOT NULL\n);\n```\n\n`init` creates it for you if it is not there already, and leaves it alone if it is:\n\n```sh\nexport DB_URL='jdbc:h2:./auth-demo'\njava -cp out:/tmp/h2/h2.jar AuthenticationApp init\n```\n\nOutput:\n\n```\nDatabase schema is ready.\n```\n\nEmail addresses cannot repeat, because the address is the table's key. If you prefer to create the\ntable yourself, run the SQL above - the program does not need `init`, it only needs a table called\n`app_user` with those two columns.\n\n## Saving an account\n\n`add` takes an email address and hashes a password for it. The password is read from an environment\nvariable when that variable is set (handy for scripts); otherwise the program asks you to type it,\nand what you type is not shown on screen.\n\n```sh\nDEMO_PASSWORD='correct horse battery staple' \\\n  java -cp out:/tmp/h2/h2.jar AuthenticationApp add alice@example.com\n```\n\nOutput:\n\n```\nPassword stored.\n```\n\n## Signing in\n\n```sh\nDEMO_PASSWORD='correct horse battery staple' \\\n  java -cp out:/tmp/h2/h2.jar AuthenticationApp login alice@example.com\n```\n\n```\nAuthentication successful.\n```\n\nGet the password wrong, or use an address nobody registered, and you get the other line:\n\n```sh\nDEMO_PASSWORD='wrong' \\\n  java -cp out:/tmp/h2/h2.jar AuthenticationApp login alice@example.com\n```\n\n```\nAuthentication unsuccessful.\n```\n\nThis is on purpose: the program never tells you whether the address exists or whether just the\npassword was wrong. That difference is what attackers use to collect lists of real accounts.\n\n## Options and exit status\n\n| Option | Meaning |\n| --- | --- |\n| `--url <jdbc-url>` | where the database is. Also read from `DB_URL`. Default `jdbc:h2:./auth-demo` |\n| `--db-user <name>` | database user. Also read from `DB_USER`. Default `sa` |\n| `--password-env <VAR>` | which environment variable to read the **sign-in** password from. Default `DEMO_PASSWORD` |\n| `--help` | print the list of commands |\n\nThe **database's own** password is read only from the environment variable `DB_PASSWORD`, never\nfrom a command line argument, because arguments show up in other people's `ps` output and in shell\nhistory.\n\n`login` also answers through its exit status, so scripts can branch on it:\n\n| Exit status | Meaning |\n| --- | --- |\n| `0` | accepted - the email address and password go together |\n| `1` | refused - wrong password, unknown address, or the database could not be read |\n| `2` | the address or password was not in a form the program accepts |\n| `3` | usage problem, or the account could not be saved |\n\nAnything written to standard error is for the person running the program. The decision itself is\nalways exactly one line on standard output.\n\n## How the check works\n\n1. **The address is tidied.** Spaces around it are removed and it is folded to lower case, so\n   `Alice@Example.com` and `alice@example.com` are the same account. The same tidy-up happens when\n   an account is saved and when it is looked up, which is what makes the two agree. Addresses that\n   are not shaped like addresses are refused straight away, without touching the database.\n2. **One question is asked of the database**, and only about the address:\n\n   ```sql\n   SELECT password_hash FROM app_user WHERE email = ?\n   ```\n\n   The `?` is filled in by JDBC as a *value*, never pasted into the sentence itself. That is what\n   stops the classic trick where somebody types an email address of `' OR '1'='1` and talks the\n   database into handing over a row. The same applies when saving an account.\n3. **The password never goes to the database.** Only the email address is sent. The stored value is\n   compared with the typed password here, in the program.\n4. **The comparison decides it.** If the password reproduces the stored hash the answer is\n   successful, otherwise unsuccessful.\n\n## How passwords are stored\n\nPasswords are never stored as text. What goes into `password_hash` looks like this:\n\n```\npbkdf2-sha256$120000$<16 random bytes>$<32 byte result>\n```\n\n* **PBKDF2 with HMAC-SHA256**, run 120,000 times. It is deliberately slow, which makes guessing\n  passwords from a stolen copy of the table expensive.\n* **A fresh random 16 byte salt per account**, so two people who choose the same password get\n  different stored values, and a list of pre-computed guesses is useless.\n* **The cost is written into the stored value.** A future version can raise the number for new\n  accounts while still being able to check old ones.\n* Only the result is kept. There is no way to read a password back out of it.\n\nA stored value that is not in that shape - a plain-text password someone pasted in, a truncated\nhash, a made-up cost - is always refused rather than trusted.\n\nThe program refuses some input rather than quietly doing something surprising with it: an empty\npassword, a password longer than 1024 characters, and passwords containing a NUL character or an\nunpaired half of a surrogate pair. Those last two matter because Java's password-key class maps\nthem onto *other* passwords, so two different passwords could end up sharing one stored value.\n\n## Two things worth knowing about the timing\n\nDeciding \"no such account\" and deciding \"wrong password\" take the same amount of time. When no row\nmatches, the program still does a full hash calculation before answering. If it answered instantly\nfor unknown addresses, an attacker could time the responses to find out which email addresses have\naccounts here. The checks measure this: on this machine both refusals took about 176 ms.\n\nInput that is refused before the database is asked (an empty password, a malformed address) comes\nback fast, which does leak a little - it says only that what was typed is not a valid address or\npassword shape, which the person typing it already knows.\n\n## If the database cannot be reached\n\nThe sign-in is refused, never granted. A database that is down, a wrong connection URL, a missing\ndriver - all of them end with `Authentication unsuccessful.` and exit status 1, with the reason on\nstandard error. Saving an account in the same situation reports exit status 3, because there the\ncaller needs to know the write did not happen.\n\n## Running the checks\n\n```sh\n./run-tests.sh\n```\n\nThe script compiles the program and the checks and runs them. It looks for the driver at\n`/tmp/h2/h2.jar`; point it somewhere else with `H2_JAR=/path/to/your.jar ./run-tests.sh`. The last\nline reads `RESULT: PASSED` and the exit status is 0 when everything holds.\n\nThe checks cover, among other things:\n\n* the happy path and every way to get it wrong, run against real in-memory H2 databases;\n* the whole program driven as a separate process, checking the exact line it prints and the exit\n  status it returns;\n* quoting attacks (`' OR '1'='1`, `'; DROP TABLE app_user; --`) and wildcard addresses (`%`, `_`),\n  proving they cannot sign in and never reach the SQL text;\n* a fake JDBC driver that records the exact SQL sentences and bound values, so a value being\n  pasted into a sentence instead of bound to it is caught immediately;\n* stored values in every wrong shape - plain text, truncated, wrong cost, wrong algorithm name -\n  none of which may open an account;\n* both refusals taking the same time, so the difference cannot be used to list accounts.\n\nBoth kinds of database check are used on purpose. The fake driver sees the SQL text; a real engine\nsees a predicate that now matches too much (replacing `email = ?` with `email LIKE ?` slips past the\nfake driver and is caught by H2). They catch different mistakes.\n\n### Proving the checks actually bite\n\nA suite that passes proves nothing until you know it would notice a broken program.\n`tools/mutation-check.py` injects 22 deliberate defects - the lookup comparing with `LIKE`, the\naddress pasted into the SQL text instead of bound, the password stored as text, one salt for every\naccount, a broken database reported as a signed-in session, a refusal exiting 0, and so on - and\nreports whether the suite fails because of each one:\n\n```sh\npython3 tools/mutation-check.py\n```\n\nAll 22 are currently killed, so every one of those defects would be caught. Two of them were\nsurvivors when the checks were first written, which is how two weak checks were found and replaced:\nthe fixtures for \"stored hash of the wrong length\" and \"stored cost below the floor\" were each broken\nin a *second* way as well, so a different guard rejected them and the guard under test was never\nreached. The replacement fixtures are invalid in exactly one way.\n\n## Handy flags for demo or test databases\n\nIf you want a throwaway database that lives only while the program runs, add the H2 in-memory flag:\n\n```sh\nDB_URL='jdbc:h2:mem:demo;DB_CLOSE_DELAY=-1' java -cp out:/tmp/h2/h2.jar AuthenticationApp init\n```\n\nTo keep several test runs apart on the same machine, give each its own URL.\n\n## Pointing it at a real database\n\nNothing here is H2 specific. For PostgreSQL, for example:\n\n```sh\nexport DB_URL='jdbc:postgresql://db.example.com:5432/accounts'\nexport DB_USER='accounts_app'\nexport DB_PASSWORD='...'\njava -cp out:/path/to/postgresql.jar AuthenticationApp login alice@example.com\n```\n\nGive that user permission on `app_user` and nothing else. The program reads one table and writes one\nrow at a time; it never needs to create or drop anything unless you run `init`.\n"}]}},{"type":"user","uuid":"3a6deb19-55df-4946-814b-753445fa0420","session_id":"4621c4dd-569f-4b1f-97f3-783dbc4fa71c","parent_tool_use_id":null,"message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"tool_result","tool_use_id":"call_991d6ae448a14b77a98e9c35","is_error":false,"content":"#!/bin/sh\n# Compiles the program and its checks, then runs the checks.\n#\n#   ./run-tests.sh\n#\n# Only two things are needed: a JDK 17 or later, and an H2 driver jar.\nset -e\ncd \"$(dirname \"$0\")\"\n\nH2_JAR=${H2_JAR:-/tmp/h2/h2.jar}\nif [ ! -f \"$H2_JAR\" ]; then\n  echo \"H2 driver not found at $H2_JAR - set H2_JAR to the path of your driver jar.\" >&2\n  echo \"Any JDBC driver works for the program itself; the checks use this one.\" >&2\n  exit 3\nfi\n\nrm -rf out\nmkdir -p out\n\njavac -d out src/AuthenticationApp.java\njavac -nowarn -cp \"out:$H2_JAR\" -d out test/AuthenticationAppTests.java\n\njava -Dtest.classes=\"$PWD/out\" -Dh2.jar=\"$H2_JAR\" -cp \"out:$H2_JAR\" AuthenticationAppTests\n"}]}},{"type":"assistant","uuid":"6e7f3192-fb84-4d6f-9ba5-5115b6aaa2d7","session_id":"4621c4dd-569f-4b1f-97f3-783dbc4fa71c","parent_tool_use_id":null,"message":{"id":"6e7f3192-fb84-4d6f-9ba5-5115b6aaa2d7","type":"message","role":"assistant","model":"vllm-general","content":[{"type":"text","text":"This code appears to be very hardened. The EqualWork params' expectedHash is the hash of \"this value is never a real password\". Could a user supply that literal string as password and authenticate? The salt in EqualWork.PARAMS is a random 16 bytes generated at class load and is NOT in the database. For `matches` to return true, we need `derive(password, EqualWork.salt, 120000, 32)` == EqualWork.expectedHash. That happens exactly when password == \"this value is never a real password\"! \n\nSo: if the account does not exist (storedHash == null) OR the stored hash is malformed, and the user submits the password `this value is never a real password`, then `matches()` returns **true** → `AuthResult.SUCCESS` → \"Authentication successful.\"\n\nWait — but does that mean authentication succeeds for a nonexistent account? Yes! `authenticate` returns `passwordMatches ? SUCCESS : FAILURE`. If `PasswordHasher.matches` returns true, we get SUCCESS for an account that doesn't exist. That's an authentication bypass!\n\nLet me double check: `parse(null)` returns null → params = EqualWork.PARAMS. EqualWork.PARAMS = parse(hash(\"this value is never a real password\".toCharArray())). `hash()` returns `pbkdf2-sha256$120000$<salt>$<hash>`. parse of that gives salt = random 16 bytes, iterations = 120000, expectedHash = PBKDF2(\"this value is never a real password\", salt, 120000, 256).\n\nThen in matches: candidate = derive(password, params.salt, params.iterations, 32). If password is exactly \"this value is never a real password\", candidate == expectedHash → MessageDigest.isEqual → true → SUCCESS.\n\nIs \"this value is never a real password\" usable by isUsable? Length 37, no NUL, no surrogates → yes, usable. And email must be a valid shape (any non-existent address). The equal-work stand-in deliberately compares against \"a hash this password cannot possibly produce\" — the comment is wrong: the password CAN produce it, namely the literal constant string. So the flaw is that a known, hardcoded password value is used to build the decoy hash, and that same known plaintext is accepted as a matching credential. CWE-287 Improper Authentication (primary). Also relates to CWE-798 hard-coded credentials. I'll report CWE-287 as primary with CWE-798 mentioned... The report format wants one CWE per finding in the summary table. This skill covers building an automated crypto audit tool that scans Python and configuration files for common cryptographic weaknesses.\n\n\n## When to Use\n\n- When conducting security assessments that involve performing cryptographic audit of application\n- When following incident response procedures for related security events\n- When performing scheduled security testing or auditing I'll fold this into SEC on env var as part of the same finding rather than a separate one.\n\n4. **Timing side channel?** Let me check more carefully whether the equal-work claim actually holds, since there could be a real timing leak.\n\n- Unknown account: derive with 120,000 iterations, 16-byte salt, 32-byte out.\n- Known account (hash written by this program): 120,000 iterations, 16-byte salt, 32-byte out.\nEqual. PASS (assuming all rows created by `add`).\n\nBut: `parse()` accepts iterations from the stored value in [1000, 2e6]. If any row has 1,000 iterations, that lookup is ~120x faster → timing oracle revealing account existence AND that the row is weak. Only relevant if rows exist with non-default cost. Minor. Also `salt.length < 8 || > 64` accepted → work nearly identical.\n\nAlso: INVALID_INPUT path returns instantly (before DB) — README acknowledges this. It reveals only input-shape invalidity, which the caller supplied. Fine.\n\nAlso: `Emails.normalize` rejects malformed emails fast → fast path. Documented.\n\nHmm, one more timing consideration: `MessageDigest.isEqual` — constant time. PASS.\n\nAnother real one: **`derive` throws IllegalStateException** (wrapped) for GeneralSecurityException → caught in AuthService as RuntimeException → FAILURE. Fail-closed. PASS.\n\n5. **Default DB credentials / no-auth DB default** — `sa` + empty password + file DB, and `--url` accepts arbitrary JDBC URL. CWE-1188 (Exposure of Resource with Weak Default Password)? Hmm — but there IS a real angle: H2 `INIT` in JDBC URL is a known RCE vector when the URL is attacker-influenced. Not attacker-influenced here. What about the regex allowing `.` at start/end, `..`, and domain starting with `-`? Permissive but no security impact given consistency (no injection since parameterised, no host routing). PASS-ish. \n\nHmm, one subtle thing: `trimmed.length() > MAX_EMAIL_CHARS` (254) checked before regex, and VARCHAR(254) column. normalize can't grow (toLowerCase(ROOT) preserves length for ASCII; for non-ASCII, toLowerCase can change length! e.g. 'İ' (U+0130) lowercases to \"i̇\" 2 chars in some locales — with ROOT locale, 'İ' → \"i̇\" (U+0069 U+0307), which is 2 chars! So a 254-char input could exceed 254 after lowering... but the input regex check happens after lowering and requires [a-z0-9...] only, so any non-ASCII → regex fails → rejected. And a non-ASCII input that lowers to ASCII? Turkish dotless İ→ no. Only ASCII-uppercase A-Z lowers into the allowed set; any other uppercase (e.g. 'À') lowers to 'à' which fails regex. So no overflow. Also could a >254 input pass regex? No, length checked before lowering; lowering could shrink (ß→? no, ß stays). Actually length check is on `trimmed` BEFORE lowering. Lowering can only grow for certain chars (İ, ﬁ ligature → \"fi\" shrinks? ﬁ U+FB01 lowercases to itself). Growth cases: 'İ'→2 chars. But such input fails the regex anyway. So no DB truncation risk. Executive Summary\n\nReviewed the whole folder: the 592-line Java program in `src/`, its 179-check test suite in `test/`, the 22-mutant mutation harness in `tools/`, `run-tests.sh`, and `README.md`.\n\nThe SQL layer is genuinely safe — every value that reaches the database is bound as a parameter, and the table name is a compile-time constant. Credential hygiene is also good: PBKDF2 with a per-account random salt, constant-time digest comparison, `char[]` wiping, and a fail-closed database path.\n\n**One critical defect undoes all of that.** To make \"no such account\" and \"wrong password\" take the same time, `PasswordHasher.matches` substitutes a stand-in hash built by hashing a literal string that is written in the source: `\"this value is never a real password\"`. That literal is a password, and the substituted parameters are a real hash of it — so the literal opens **every account whose stored value is unreadable, and any sign-in attempt for an account that has no row**, which is exactly the case the stand-in was meant to protect. Verified end-to-end against a real H2 database: `login attacker@example.com` (no such user) with `DEMO_PASSWORD='this value is never a real password'` prints `Authentication successful.` and exits 0.\n\nNone of the 179 checks, and none of the 22 mutants, catch this: the test at `test/AuthenticationAppTests.java:115` asserts the null-stored-hash path with `\"hunter2\"`, never with the literal.\n\nFour further findings are reported: a caller-controlled JDBC connection string that verifiably executes arbitrary SQL at connect time, no password strength requirement at enrolment, a PBKDF2 cost floor 120x below the stated policy with no upgrade path, and driver error text (including connection detail) copied to stderr.\n\nVerified by compiling to a scratch directory and driving the real CLI with a real H2 driver, so every \"verified\" claim below is observed behaviour, not inference. Nothing in this folder was modified.\n\n## 2. Scope\n\n| File | Purpose | Security-relevant behaviour |\n| --- | --- | --- |\n| `src/AuthenticationApp.java` | The program: `init`, `add <email>`, `login <email>` against one `app_user` table. | Reads sign-in password from env/console/stdin (`:166`-`:181`); builds a JDBC connection from caller-supplied URL/user with an empty default password (`:79`-`:82`); binds SQL parameters (`:373`, `:387`); hashes with PBKDF2-SHA256 and compares digests (`:461`-`:556`); rejects malformed stored values (`:513`-`:542`); refuses sign-in on database error (`:304`-`:307`); prints a hardcoded equal-work decoy hash derived from a source literal (`:507`-`:510`). |\n| `test/AuthenticationAppTests.java` | 179 checks; drives the program as a separate process against real in-memory H2 and a recording fake driver. | Asserts bound-parameter SQL text, exact stdout line, exit status, malformed-stored-value rejection, refusal-time equality. Spawns processes with `ProcessBuilder` using argument lists and per-run temp directories (`:731`-`:736`) — no shell interpolation. Blind to the hardcoded decoy password (`:115`). |\n| `tools/mutation-check.py` | Injects 22 deliberate defects and reports whether the suite notices each. | Mutates only the single source file inside a fresh `tempfile.mkdtemp` tree and removes it afterwards; every `subprocess.run` uses an argument list with no `shell=True`; no network access. Also invites operators to append arbitrary H2 flags to `DB_URL`, which is relevant to SEC-02. |\n| `out/` | Prebuilt `.class` files. | Build output; not reviewed as source and left untouched (the check script recreates it). |\n\n## 3. This is the skill that caught SEC-01 (\"hardcoded keys/passwords in source code\") and SEC-04 (\"weak KDF / low iteration count accepted\").\n- **`testing-api-authentication-weaknesses`** — applied to the sign-in path end to end (`AuthenticationApp.run` `:79`-`:116`, `AuthService.authenticate` `:293`-`:320`, `Database.findPasswordHash` `:386`-`:401`): token/credential validation, credential policy, account-enumeration leakage, and whether any input reaches the verifier without being checked. Produced SEC-01, SEC-03 and the enumeration assessment.\n- **`exploiting-sql-injection-vulnerabilities`** — applied to `Database.initSchema`/`addUser`/`findPasswordHash` (`:347`-`:401`), plus the attacker-controlled inputs named in that skill (email, stored hash): string-concatenated SQL, second-order risk from stored values, `LIKE` vs `=` semantics, and wildcard quoting. Produced SEC-01. Also confirmed no database credentials, connection strings, or signing keys are committed anywhere in the folder.\n- **`testing-for-broken-access-control`** — applied to `login`/`add` for object-level and function-level authorization (`:363`-`:376`, `:386`-`:401`): whether one address can read or overwrite another's row, and whether `add` can be used to displace an existing credential. Checked, no finding — the address is the primary key, a duplicate insert is rejected (`:187`-`:194`, `:395`-`:397`), and there is no per-object surface in a single-tenant CLI.\n\nReviewed against general OWASP Top 10 / CWE knowledge because no skill in the library matched the behaviour:\n\n- **Trust of a caller-supplied JDBC connection string** and driver-specific URL parameters (`:79`-`:82`, `:344`-`:345`) → SEC-02.\n- **Information exposure through error messages** (`:192`) → SEC-05.\n- **Absence of a password strength requirement at enrolment** (`:368`-`:371`) → SEC-03 (the library has no credential-policy skill).\n- **Regular-expression safety of `EMAIL_PATTERN`** (`:568`-`:569`) — a single linear scan over input already capped at 254 characters, no nested quantifiers, so no catastrophic backtracking. Summary of Findings\n\n| ID | Title | Severity | CWE | Location |\n| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |\n| SEC-01 | Hardcoded decoy password authenticates whenever the stored hash is missing or malformed | Critical | CWE-287 | `src/AuthenticationApp.java:509` |\n| SEC-02 | JDBC connection string and driver parameters are trusted without validation | Medium | CWE-20 | `src/AuthenticationApp.java:80` |\n| SEC-03 | No password strength requirement at enrolment; single-character passwords accepted | Medium | CWE-521 | `src/AuthenticationApp.java:368` |\n| SEC-04 | Accepted PBKDF2 cost floor (1,000 iterations) is 120x below policy, with no rehash-on-login | Medium | CWE-916 | `src/AuthenticationApp.java:422` |\n| SEC-05 | Database driver error text, including connection detail, copied to stderr | Low | CWE-209 | `src/AuthenticationApp.java:192` |\n\n## 5. Detailed Findings\n\n### SEC-01: Hardcoded decoy password authenticates whenever the stored hash is missing or malformed\n\n- **Severity:** Critical\n- **CWE:** CWE-287: Improper Authentication (root cause CWE-321: Use of Hard-coded Cryptographic Key / CWE-798: Use of Hard-coded Credentials)\n- **Location:** `src/AuthenticationApp.java:509` (with `:484` and `:479`-`:492`)\n\n**Vulnerable code:**\n\n```java\n// src/AuthenticationApp.java:507-510\n    /** One real hash, calculated once, used as the equal-effort stand-in for a missing account. */\n    private static final class EqualWork {\n        static final Params PARAMS = parse(hash(\"this value is never a real password\".toCharArray()));\n    }\n```\n\n```java\n// src/AuthenticationApp.java:479-492\n    static boolean boolean matches(char[] password, String storedHash) {\n        Params params = parse(storedHash);\n        if (params == null) {\n            // Unknown account or unreadable stored value: spend the same effort as a real check\n            // and compare against a hash this password cannot possibly produce.\n            params = EqualWork.PARAMS;\n        }\n        byte[] candidate = derive(password, params.salt, params.iterations, params.expectedHash.length);\n        try {\n            return MessageDigest.isEqual(candidate, params.expectedHash);\n        } finally {\n            Arrays.fill(candidate, (byte) 0);\n        }\n    }\n```\n\n**Why it is a problem:**\n\nThe comment says the comparison is made \"against a hash this password cannot possibly produce\". It is the opposite. `EqualWork.PARAMS` is `parse(hash(LITERAL))`, so it holds a *valid* salt, iteration count and digest **of the literal string itself**. Substituting it means: for any input where `parse(storedHash)` returns null, the verifier accepts the literal from the source code as that account's password.\n\n`parse` returns null for `storedHash == null` (`:514`-`:516`) — every sign-in attempt for an address nobody registered — and for every malformed stored value (`:518`-`:540`). So the constant is not a stand-in, it is a **global backdoor password** for the whole class of failed lookups. An attacker does not need to crack or guess anything; the credential is printed in the source, and the comment names it.\n\nThe claim in `README.md` — \"A stored value that is not in that shape - a plain-text password someone pasted in, a truncated hash, a made-up cost - is always refused rather than trusted\" — is false for precisely one password.\n\nVerified against a real H2 database with the compiled program:\n\n```text\n$ DEMO_PASSWORD='this value is never a real password' \\\n    java -cp <classes>:<h2.jar> AuthenticationApp --url 'jdbc:h2:file:…' login attacker@example.com\nAuthentication successful.          exit=0     (no such user exists)\n\n$ DEMO_PASSWORD='whatever' … login attacker@example.com\nAuthentication unsuccessful.        exit=1     (control)\n\n# existing account whose stored value is not a valid hash (\"plaintext-paste-123\"):\n$ DEMO_PASSWORD='this value is never a real password' … login bob@example.com\nAuthentication successful.          exit=0\n```\n\nImpact: authentication bypass. Reach: anyone who can read the source, any container image, any jar (the literal survives compilation as an ordinary string constant — it is visible with `strings`), and anyone who copies this file into another service, which is how such constants spread. It also silently converts \"stored value corrupted\" into \"account openable with a published password\".\n\n**Fix:**\n\nDo not derive the equal-effort parameters from a password at all. Use fixed salt bytes and an expected digest that no input can produce, so the decoy is a cost schedule rather than a credential:\n\n```java\n// Fixed, public, and deliberately not a password: a cost schedule plus a 32-byte expectation\n// that is all zero. No PBKDF2 output is assumed to be 32 zero bytes, so the comparison can\n// never succeed, whatever the caller types.\nprivate static final class EqualWork {\n    private static final byte[] FIXED_SALT = {\n        (byte) 0x00, (byte) 0x01, (byte) 0x02, (byte) 0x03, (byte) 0x04, (byte) 0x05, (byte) 0x06, (byte) 0x07,\n        (byte) 0x08, (byte) 0x09, (byte) 0x0a, (byte) 0x0b, (byte) 0x0c, (byte) 0x0d, (byte) 0x0e, (byte) 0x0f\n    };\n\n    static final Params PARAMS = new Params(\n            FIXED_SALT.clone(), ITERATIONS, new byte[HASH_BYTES]);\n}\n```\n\nIf the timing of the malformed-value path must stay identical to the unknown-account path, give `matches` a third input instead of smuggling a credential through `parse` — that is, distinguish \"no row\" from \"unusable row\" in `AuthService.authenticate` (`:315`) and pass the decoy `Params` explicitly. A malformed stored value should fail loudly, not be hashed against a placeholder:\n\n```java\n// src/AuthenticationApp.java:479-485 — after the fix\nstatic boolean matches(char[] password, String storedHash) {\n    Params params = parse(storedHash);\n    if (params == null) {\n        // Nothing readable was stored for this address: no password can match it.\n        return false;\n    }\n```\n\nAdd the check the suite is missing, and a mutant that re-introduces this defect, so the blind spot closes permanently:\n\n```java\n// test/AuthenticationAppTests.java — alongside the existing null-stored-hash check at :115\ncheck(\"the equal-work decoy string never authenticates against a missing stored hash\",\n        !PasswordHasher.matches(\n                \"this value is never a real password\".toCharArray(), null));\n```\n\n```python\n# tools/mutation-check.py — kills any future reintroduction, unlike M08 which only removes the work\n(\"M23-decoy-is-a-password\",\n \"derive the equal-effort stand-in from a real password literal\",\n \"new Params(FIXED_SALT.clone(), ITERATIONS, new byte[HASH_BYTES]);\",\n \"parse(hash(\\\"this value is never a real password\\\".toCharArray()));\"),\n```\n\n### SEC-02: JDBC connection string and driver parameters are trusted without validation\n\n- **Severity:** Medium\n- **CWE:** CWE-20: Improper Input Validation (enabling CWE-94/CWE-502 via driver URL parameters, and CWE-319 by not requiring transport security)\n- **Location:** `src/AuthenticationApp.java:80` (with `:81`-`:82` and `:344`-`:345`)\n\n**Vulnerable code:**\n\n```java\n// src/AuthenticationApp.java:79-83\n        Database db = new Database(\n                a.optionOrEnv(\"--url\", \"DB_URL\", \"jdbc:h2:./auth-demo\"),\n                a.optionOrEnv(\"--db-user\", \"DB_USER\", \"sa\"),\n                firstOrNull(System.getenv(\"DB_PASSWORD\"), \"\"));\n        String passwordEnvironmentVariable = firstOrNull(a.option(\"--password-env\"), \"DEMO_PASSWORD\");\n```\n\n```java\n// src/AuthenticationApp.java:344-345\n    private Connection open() throws SQLException {\n        return DriverManager.getConnection(url, user, password);\n    }\n```\n\n**Why it is a problem:**\n\nThe URL is passed to `DriverManager.getConnection` verbatim, with no scheme or host allowlist and no rejection of driver-specific parameters. `login` accepts `--url` too, so the host that decides whether a sign-in succeeds is chosen by whoever sets the option or `DB_URL`, and the default credential is user `sa` with an empty password. `README.md` actively encourages appending driver flags to `DB_URL` (`jdbc:h2:mem:demo;DB_CLOSE_DELAY=-1`), so operators will treat the string as free-form.\n\nH2's `INIT` parameter runs SQL at connect time. Verified through this program's own connection path:\n\n```text\n$ java -cp <classes>:<h2.jar> AuthenticationApp \\\n      --url 'jdbc:h2:file:/tmp/pwn;INIT=CREATE SCHEMA IF NOT EXISTS INJECTED' init\nDatabase schema is ready.          exit=0\n\n$ … -sql \"SELECT schema_name FROM information_schema.schemata\"\nSCHEMA_NAME\nINFORMATION_SCHEMA\nPUBLIC\nINJECTED            <- DDL executed at connect time, before any code in this program ran\n```\n\nI did not achieve Java execution through it: `INIT=CREATE ALIAS … FOR \"java.lang.Runtime.exec\"` was refused (exit 3). The escalation is driver- and classpath-dependent rather than demonstrated here, but the same channel is a documented code-execution route for several drivers (H2 `RUNSCRIPT FROM '<url>'`, MySQL `autoDeserialize`/`queryInterceptors`, PostgreSQL `sslfactory`/`socketFactory` class loading).\n\nTwo consequences matter for a *sign-in* program specifically. First, wherever `DB_URL` is influenced by someone with less privilege than the account the program runs as — a CI variable, a templated deployment config, a shared wrapper script, an over-permissive `.env` — that person can run arbitrary SQL as the database user and can drop/alter the table the program owns (it connects as a user that `init` assumes may create schema). Second, because the whole authentication decision is delegated to the connected database, pointing `--url`/`DB_URL` at an attacker-supplied database lets them choose the stored hashes, and any password they enrol for an address is accepted for the real address — a straightforward bypass that needs no bug in the hashing code.\n\nThe empty default for `DB_PASSWORD` (`:82`) means a misconfigured deployment silently connects anonymously rather than refusing to run, and nothing anywhere enforces TLS for a remote database.\n\n**Fix:**\n\nConstrain the target, refuse to run on a configuration that is not explicitly provided, and require TLS for non-local hosts:\n\n```java\n// src/AuthenticationApp.java:79-82 — replace\n        String url = a.optionOrEnv(\"--url\", \"DB_URL\", null);\n        String dbUser = a.optionOrEnv(\"--db-user\", \"DB_USER\", null);\n        String dbPassword = System.getenv(\"DB_PASSWORD\");\n        if (url == null || dbUser == null || dbPassword == null) {\n            System.err.println(\"[app] refusing to start: DB_URL, DB_USER and DB_PASSWORD must all be set\");\n            usage(System.err);\n            return 3;\n        }\n        if (!isAllowedDatabase(url)) {\n            System.err.println(\"[app] refusing this database URL: the scheme, host and parameters must \"\n                    + \"match the allowlist in DB_URL_ALLOW_PREFIX\");\n            return 3;\n        }\n        Database db = new Database(url, dbUser, dbPassword);\n```\n\n```java\n// new helper next to firstOrNull (src/AuthenticationApp.java:195)\n    /** Prefix allowlist plus a hard ban on driver parameters that execute or load code. */\n    private static boolean isAllowedDatabase(String url) {\n        String prefixes = System.getenv(\"DB_URL_ALLOW_PREFIX\");   // e.g. \"jdbc:postgresql://db.internal:5432/\"\n        if (prefixes == null || prefixes.isBlank()) {\n            return false;\n        }\n        String lower = url.toLowerCase(java.util.Locale.ROOT);\n        boolean prefixOk = java.util.Arrays.stream(prefixes.split(\",\"))\n                .map(String::trim)\n                .filter(s -> !s.isEmpty())\n                .anyMatch(lower::startsWith);\n        if (!prefixOk) {\n            return false;\n        }\n        // Driver features that turn a connection string into code execution or filesystem access.\n        for (String banned : new String[] { \"init=\", \"runscript\", \"shutdown=\", \"autodeserialize\",\n                                            \"queryinterceptors\", \"allowloadlocalinfile\", \"sslfactory\",\n                                            \"socketfactory\", \"initscript=\" }) {\n            if (lower.contains(banned)) {\n                return false;\n            }\n        }\n        // Anything not on this host must be encrypted.\n        return lower.startsWith(\"jdbc:postgresql:\") || lower.startsWith(\"jdbc:sqlserver:\")\n                || lower.startsWith(\"jdbc:mysql:\") || lower.startsWith(\"jdbc:h2:\");\n    }\n```\n\nThen drop the `sa` default, and make the schema-owning `init` a separately-invoked path with its own, more privileged connection, so the account used by `login` only needs `SELECT` on `app_user` — which is what `README.md` already recommends and the code does not enforce.\n\n### SEC-03: No password strength requirement at enrolment; single-character passwords accepted\n\n- **Severity:** Medium\n- **CWE:** CWE-521: Weak Password Requirements\n- **Location:** `src/AuthenticationApp.java:368`-`:371` (policy surface at `:438`-`:458`)\n\n**Vulnerable code:**\n\n```java\n// src/AuthenticationApp.java:363-373\n    boolean addUser(String email, char[] password) throws SQLException {\n        String normalizedEmail = Emails.normalize(email);\n        if (normalizedEmail == null) {\n            throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"that is not an email address this program accepts\");\n        }\n        if (!PasswordHasher.isUsable(password)) {\n            throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"the password must be 1 to \"\n                    + PasswordHasher.MAX_PASSWORD_CHARS + \" characters and may not contain a NUL\");\n        }\n        String hash = PasswordHasher.hash(password);\n```\n\n**Why it is a problem:**\n\n`addUser` — the only enrolment path in the program — applies no strength requirement whatsoever. `isUsable` is a *safety* check (bounded length, no NUL, no unpaired surrogates), and its own message advertises the minimum as **1 character**. Verified:\n\n```text\n$ DEMO_PASSWORD='a' … AuthenticationApp … add weak@example.com\nPassword stored.                                          exit=0\n$ DEMO_PASSWORD='a' … AuthenticationApp … login weak@example.com\nAuthentication successful.                                exit=0\n# stored value: pbkdf2-sha256$120000$jDF+nDs8jqg49UxIWqR…   (correctly hashed, meaninglessly so)\n```\n\nPBKDF2 at 120,000 iterations protects the *table*, not the *account*. Against a one-character or dictionary password the cost factor is irrelevant: the whole single-key-space is searched in well under a second per account, and 120,000 iterations is only ~10k guesses/second/core on one machine — an attacker who has dumped the table, or who simply targets one known address, is in immediately. The program advertises itself as the credential store for an application (`README.md`: \"Pointing it at a real database\"), so the missing policy is the weakest link in the design, and there is no bypass anywhere else to blame: nothing rate-limits this path either, because it is the enrolment path.\n\n**Fix:**\n\nEnforce the policy where the credential is created, and keep `isUsable` as the shape check it already is:\n\n```java\n// src/AuthenticationApp.java:368-371 — replace\n        if (!PasswordHasher.isUsable(password)) {\n            throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"the password must be \" + MIN_PASSWORD_CHARS + \" to \"\n                    + PasswordHasher.MAX_PASSWORD_CHARS + \" characters and may not contain a NUL\");\n        }\n        if (!PasswordPolicy.isAcceptable(password)) {\n            throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"the password is too weak to store: use at least \"\n                    + MIN_PASSWORD_CHARS + \" characters and do not reuse a breached password\");\n        }\n```\n\n```java\n// new class alongside PasswordHasher (src/AuthenticationApp.java)\n/** Policy for credentials being stored. Deliberately length-first, per NIST SP 800-63B. */\nfinal class PasswordPolicy {\n\n    private static final int MIN_CHARS = 15;\n    private static final java.util.Set<String> BLOCKED = java.util.Set.of(\n            \"password\", \"passwort\", \"qwerty\", \"letmein\", \"changeme\", \"welcome\", \"admin\");\n\n    private PasswordPolicy() {\n    }\n\n    static boolean isAcceptable(char[] password) {\n        String lower = new String(password).toLowerCase(java.util.Locale.ROOT);\n        if (password.length < MIN_CHARS) {\n            return false;\n        }\n        // Reject a credential that is one of the common forms, with trivial affixes.\n        for (String blocked : BLOCKED) {\n            if (lower.contains(blocked)) {\n                return false;\n            }\n        }\n        // Reject a credential with no character variety at all (\"aaaaaaaaaaaaaaa\").\n        return lower.chars().distinct().count() >= 5;\n    }\n}\n```\n\nFor a real deployment, replace the local blocklist with an HIBP range query (k-anonymity, no password leaves the host in the clear) and log a rejection without recording the value. Note this is a policy decision, not just code: pick `MIN_CHARS` to match whoever's account table this becomes, and check the login path keeps using `isUsable` only, so existing accounts are never locked out by a policy change.\n\n### SEC-04: Accepted PBKDF2 cost floor (1,000 iterations) is 120x below policy, with no rehash-on-login\n\n- **Severity:** Medium\n- **CWE:** CWE-916: Insufficient Computational Effort in Password Hashing (CWE-326: Inadequate Encryption Strength)\n- **Location:** `src/AuthenticationApp.java:422` (enforced at `:527`-`:529`; policy at `:419`)\n\n**Vulnerable code:**\n\n```java\n// src/AuthenticationApp.java:419-423\n    private static final int ITERATIONS = 120_000;\n    private static final int SALT_BYTES = 16;\n    private static final int HASH_BYTES = 32;\n    private static final int MIN_ITERATIONS = 1_000;\n    private static final int MAX_ITERATIONS = 2_000_000;\n```\n\n```java\n// src/AuthenticationApp.java:522-529\n        int iterations;\n        try {\n            iterations = Integer.parseInt(parts[1]);\n        } catch (NumberFormatException e) {\n            return null;\n        }\n        if (iterations < MIN_ITERATIONS || iterations > MAX_ITERATIONS) {\n            return null;\n        }\n```\n\n**Why it is a problem:**\n\nThe stored format carries its own cost so that it can be raised over time — a sound design — but the acceptance floor is set at 1,000 iterations, 120 times cheaper than the 120,000 the program writes, and nothing ever raises a stored value back up to policy because `login` never rehashes. Verified:\n\n```text\n# stored value written as pbkdf2-sha256$1000$…  (1,000 iterations, external tool, salt 'AAAA…')\n$ DEMO_PASSWORD='lowcostpw' … login cheap@example.com\nAuthentication successful.          exit=0\n# boundary control, pbkdf2-sha256$999$…\nAuthentication unsuccessful.        exit=1\n```\n\nThe floor is the only thing standing between a migrated, imported, or manually inserted stored value and offline cracking, and it is set low enough that the defence is nominal: at 1,000 iterations an attacker who obtains the table cracks roughly 120x faster than the design intends, per account, with no per-account cost from the salt. `README.md` frames such rows as \"a made-up cost … always refused rather than trusted\", which suggests the floor's purpose is to reject *nonsense*, not to enforce a security minimum — but `MIN_ITERATIONS` is exactly a security minimum, and a 1,000-iteration hash is well below any current recommendation (OWASP recommends 600,000 for PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA256; 120,000 is already dated).\n\nAn attacker cannot use this to pick a cost for a victim's account — writing to the table already implies full control — so the realistic exposure is (a) credentials imported from a legacy store or hand-inserted during operations silently weakening the whole table, and (b) a future cost *reduction* being accepted forever rather than upgraded. That is why this is Medium and not High.\n\n**Fix:**\n\nRaise the floor to the current policy and upgrade stored values on the next successful sign-in, so the low-cost population drains instead of accumulating:\n\n```java\n// src/AuthenticationApp.java:422 — replace\n    /** Stored values cheaper than this are not accepted, so the floor cannot be used to\n        downgrade an account. Raise it together with ITERATIONS, never below it. */\n    private static final int MIN_ITERATIONS = ITERATIONS;\n```\n\n```java\n// src/AuthenticationApp.java:479-492 — add a rehash signal so login can migrate rows\n    /** True when a stored value's cost is below the current policy and should be rewritten. */\n    static boolean needsRehash(String storedHash) {\n        Params params = parse(storedHash);\n        return params != null && params.iterations < ITERATIONS;\n    }\n```\n\n```java\n// src/AuthenticationApp.java — after a successful check in AuthService.authenticate (:315-319)\n        if (passwordMatches && PasswordHasher.needsRehash(storedHash)) {\n            // Correct password: rewrite at the current cost while the plaintext is in hand.\n            db.replacePasswordHash(normalizedEmail, PasswordHasher.hash(password));\n        }\n        return passwordMatches ? AuthResult.SUCCESS : AuthResult.FAILURE;\n```\n\nDo not lower the floor to keep old rows working — migrate them, and if some rows genuinely cannot be migrated, force a password reset for them rather than accepting a cost you would not create today. If you also move to a memory-hard function, keep the scheme name in the value (as here) so both parameters can be checked in `parse` (`:518`).\n\n### SEC-05: Database driver error text, including connection detail, copied to stderr\n\n- **Severity:** Low\n- **CWE:** CWE-209: Generation of Error Message Containing Sensitive Information\n- **Location:** `src/AuthenticationApp.java:192` (reached from `:91` and `:130`)\n\n**Vulnerable code:**\n\n```java\n// src/AuthenticationApp.java:187-195\n    private static int databaseError(SQLException e) {\n        String state = e.getSQLState() == null ? \"\" : e.getSQLState();\n        if (state.startsWith(\"23\")) {\n            System.err.println(\"[app] that email address already has credentials stored\");\n        } else {\n            System.err.println(\"[app] database error [\" + state + \"]: \" + e.getMessage());\n        }\n        return 3;\n    }\n```\n\n**Why it is a problem:**\n\n`SQLException.getMessage()` is driver-supplied and frequently echoes the connection string it was produced from — host, port, instance/database name, and sometimes the properties it carried — along with driver version and internal detail. All of it is written verbatim to stderr, which in practice means CI consoles, container logs, and log aggregators: places with much weaker access control than the deployment environment. It also hands a probing caller a cheap oracle for database type, version, and schema names (constraint names leak through the `23xxx` branch's sibling path, and `init`'s DDL failures print the failing statement).\n\nThis is Low, not Medium, because the program's security decision is unaffected: `login` still prints exactly one line on stdout and exits 1 for every database error (`:304`-`:307`), so nothing here grants access — it is disclosure only. `README.md` explicitly routes stderr to \"the person running the program\", which is a reasonable assumption for a demo and not for a service. Note the neighbouring `e.getMessage()` calls (`:53`, `:66`, `:128`) echo messages this file raises itself, so they are not a disclosure path.\n\n**Fix:**\n\nKeep the operator-usable detail behind a switch, and log only the SQL state by default:\n\n```java\n// src/AuthenticationApp.java:187-195 — replace\n    private static int databaseError(SQLException e) {\n        String state = e.getSQLState() == null ? \"\" : e.getSQLState();\n        if (state.startsWith(\"23\")) {\n            System.err.println(\"[app] that email address already has credentials stored\");\n            return 3;\n        }\n        // Driver text can echo the connection string, so it is off unless explicitly asked for.\n        if (\"1\".equals(System.getenv(\"APP_DEBUG_SQL\"))) {\n            System.err.println(\"[app] database error [\" + state + \"]: \" + e.getMessage());\n        } else {\n            System.err.println(\"[app] database error [\" + state\n                    + \"] - set APP_DEBUG_SQL=1 for the driver's own message\");\n        }\n        if (Boolean.parseBoolean(System.getenv(\"APP_LOG_SQL\"))) {\n            // Structured sink you control, not the console: strip anything URL-shaped.\n            System.getLogger(\"auth\").log(System.Logger.Level.WARNING, \"database error \" + state);\n        }\n        return 3;\n    }\n```\n\nWhatever replaces it should not put the driver's raw text into a shared log: if the full message is needed for support, write it to a local file with permissions the aggregator cannot read, and keep the console line to the SQL state.\n\n## 6. Verified Controls and Checks With No Finding\n\nConfirmed correct by reading the code and, where the claim is behavioural, by running the program against a real H2 database.\n\n- **No SQL injection.** All user-controlled values reach the database only as bound parameters — `:373`/`:387` use `?` with `setString` at `:375`-`:376` and `:389`; the concatenated fragments are the `TABLE` constant (`:332`) and static SQL, never input. DDL (`:350`-`:353`) has no input. Verified against the `exploiting-sql-injection-vulnerabilities` checklist: no concatenated predicates, no second-order path (stored values are only ever Base64-decoded and digest-compared, never re-embedded in SQL), no `ORDER BY`/identifier injection surface, no dynamic table names. Mutants `M03`/`M04` (paste the address/hash into the SQL text) and `M01`/`M02` (`UPPER()` comparison, `LIKE` instead of `=`) exist and are killed, so a regression here would be caught. Quoting attacks (`' OR '1'='1`, `'; DROP TABLE app_user; --`) and wildcard addresses (`%`, `_`) are covered as processes in the test suite and cannot reach the SQL text.\n- **Authentication decision is fail-closed.** A `SQLException` during lookup yields `AuthResult.FAILURE` (`:304`-`:307`) — exit 1, never 0 — and a `RuntimeException` while hashing also yields `FAILURE` (`:316`-`:318`). Mutants `M15` (database error reported as success), `M16` (always succeed), `M17` (success line for a refusal) and `M18` (refusal exits 0) are all killed. Observed: an unreachable database and a missing table both exit 1.\n- **Stored values are validated before use.** `parse` (`:513`-`:542`) rejects a null value, a wrong field count, a scheme other than `pbkdf2-sha256` (`:518`), a non-numeric or out-of-range cost (`:522`-`:529`), undecodable Base64 (`:531`-`:536`), a salt outside 8..64 bytes, and a digest whose length is not 32 bytes (`:538`-`:540`) — and it returns null rather than throwing, so a corrupted row cannot crash or bypass the comparison. (The consequence of `null` is SEC-01; the validation itself is sound.)\n- **Constant-time digest comparison.** `MessageDigest.isEqual(candidate, params.expectedHash)` (`:488`) rather than `Arrays.equals` or `String.equals`; the derived buffer is zeroed in a `finally` (`:489`-`:491`). Mutant `M09` (compare lengths only) is killed. No password content is compared before the digest.\n- **Salt and randomness.** `SecureRandom` (`:424`) draws a fresh 16-byte salt per stored credential (`:462`-`:463`), so identical passwords produce different values and rainbow tables do not apply; the salt is stored with the value, as it should be. Mutant `M20` (one fixed salt for every account) is killed.\n- **No plaintext password leaves the process or reaches storage.** Only the address is sent to the database. `hash` returns the derived value, never the input; `PBEKeySpec` is cleared (`spec.clearPassword()`, `:552`) and the caller's buffer is wiped via `wipe(char[])` (`:183`-`:185`) after use. Mutant `M19` (store `plain:` + password) is killed, and `test/…:76` independently asserts a stored value does not contain the password.\n- **Credential-shape checks prevent hash aliasing.** `isUsable` (`:438`-`:458`) rejects NUL characters and unpaired surrogates, which `PBEKeySpec` would otherwise fold onto other passwords so that two different inputs share one stored value; the 1024-character cap (`:415`) bounds the cost of one attempt. `MAX_PASSWORD_CHARS` is *not* a silent truncation — an over-long password is refused with exit 2, so a user cannot be locked out by an invisible cut. Mutants `M13` and `M14` are killed.\n- **Address normalisation is consistent and locale-safe.** `Emails.normalize` (`:575`-`:591`) trims (`:579`), caps at 254 (`:580`), folds with `toLowerCase(Locale.ROOT)` (`:583`) — avoiding the Turkish-I aliasing that a default-locale fold would create — shape-checks against a compiled `EMAIL_PATTERN` (`:568`-`:569`, `:585`) and limits the local part (`:587`). The same function is used when storing (`:365`) and when looking up (`:294`), which is what makes the two agree. Mutants `M06` (no lower-casing), `M07` (no trim) and `M22` (no local-part limit) are killed.\n- **No catastrophic backtracking in the email regex.** `[a-z0-9._%+-]+@[a-z0-9.-]+\\.[a-z]{2,}` (`:568`-`:569`) is a single linear pass over input already capped at 254 characters, with no nested or overlapping quantifiers; the character classes are disjoint at the `@` and `.` boundaries, so `matches` is bounded and no ReDoS is reachable.\n- **Account-enumeration timing is handled deliberately.** Both refusals perform a full 120,000-iteration derivation, so \"unknown address\" and \"wrong password\" are indistinguishable by timing (`:479`-`:492`, `:311`-`:315`) — the one defect is *which* parameters that equal work uses (SEC-01), not the equality of effort. The remaining fast path for malformed input (`:295`-`:298`) discloses only whether the submitted text is well-formed, is documented in `README.md`, and was assessed as acceptable: the submitter supplied that input and learns nothing about any account.\n- **Database errors never change the outcome** (see the fail-closed control above); stdout carries exactly one decision line for every path, so a wrapper script cannot be misled by stderr content (relevant to SEC-05).\n- **No command injection or path traversal in the supporting tooling.** `tools/mutation-check.py` applies each defect to an in-memory copy of the source, writes only inside a fresh `tempfile.mkdtemp` tree, invokes `javac`/`java` through `subprocess.run` argument lists with no `shell=True`, and removes the tree with `shutil.rmtree(..., ignore_errors=True)`; the defect descriptions are data, not code. `test/…:731`-`:736` drives the program the same way (argument list, per-run `Files.createTempDirectory` at `:623`, `DEMO_PASSWORD` set/unset via `environment().put/remove`). `run-tests.sh` uses `set -e` and quotes every expansion, including `$H2_JAR` and `$PWD`.\n- **Nothing sensitive is committed.** Swept the folder for credentials, connection strings and keys: the only hard-coded secret-shaped string in the repository is the SEC-01 literal; the default `sa`/empty-password pair (SEC-02) is a default, not a committed credential, and `README.md`'s examples use placeholders.\n- **Password transport is a considered design, not an oversight.** `readPassword` (`:166`-`:181`) prefers the named environment variable, then `Console.readPassword` (no echo), then stdin, and the database's own password is read from `DB_PASSWORD` only, never from an argument — so it does not appear in `ps` or shell history. Accepted residual: an env-var credential is visible to anything running as the same uid (`/proc/<pid>/environ`) and is inherited by child processes, which is inherent to the documented convenience rather than a defect in this code.\n","content_text":"placeholder"}}],"stop_reason":"tool_use","usage":{"input_tokens":151085,"output_tokens":10546,"cache_read_input_tokens":0,"total_tokens":161631}}},{"type":"user","uuid":"aba9dd5a-a914-4046-9b9b-8995dfbd9795","session_id":"4621c4dd-569f-4b1f-97f3-783dbc4fa71c","parent_tool_use_id":null,"message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"tool_result","tool_use_id":"call_e7294529c46f450fa67b3e6c","is_error":false,"content":"Successfully created and wrote to new file: /data/results-cyber/qwen/set_a/sallie/3/basic/security_review.md."}]}},{"type":"assistant","uuid":"db033fc0-6ceb-4018-8a39-afb3e64d5c71","session_id":"4621c4dd-569f-4b1f-97f3-783dbc4fa71c","parent_tool_use_id":null,"message":{"id":"db033fc0-6ceb-4018-8a39-afb3e64d5c71","type":"message","role":"assistant","model":"vllm-general","content":[{"type":"text","text":"I made a typo in the SEC-01 vulnerable code block: `static boolean boolean matches(char[] password, String storedHash) {` — duplicated \"boolean\".
